The store checks each again inside the write's transaction, before any DML, from its own permission read on the transaction's connection for the tables that transaction's schema read confirmed, with no catalogue read (`SqlPermissionReader.ReadResolvedAsync`, through `ReadPermissionsInTransactionAsync`, `src/EpiInfo.Data/Records/SqlRecordRepository.cs:1276-1311`): an insert once it holds the form's exclusive write lock and before its base `INSERT`, for the base table and every page table, requiring `CanInsert` (`:1110-1129`); a soft delete after its locked read finds an active record and before its stamp and its `UPDATE`, for the base table alone, requiring `CanDelete` (`:1344-1363`), while an unknown or already-deleted record reads no permission. Either refusal rolls back with nothing written and becomes the action's 403 (`RecordService.cs:395-397`, `:601-603`). `PUT` needs `canUpdate` and INSERT on every page table whose row the locked read finds missing, whether or not the request submits a field on that page. The service checks `canUpdate` on its own read before it calls the store (`:463-467`), and passes that read to the store only to turn the store's check on (`:474-482`). The store decides inside the update's transaction, after the locked read of the base row, the read of the page rows and the required gate, and before any DML: it reads the permissions again on the transaction's connection, for the base table and every page table that transaction's schema read confirmed, with no catalogue read (`SqlPermissionReader.ReadResolvedAsync`), and requires `CanUpdate` and, for each page the record read found without a row, `CanInsertInto` its table (`src/EpiInfo.Data/Records/SqlRecordRepository.cs:1221-1242`). A grant revoked while a write waited, an insert on the form's write lock or an update or a soft delete on the row lock, is therefore a refusal with nothing written, not a failed statement (SQL error 229, a 500), and a `PUT` that also fails the required gate is the gate's 400. The store's `Forbidden` result rolls back and becomes the 403 (`RecordService.cs:506-508`). The INSERT precondition is wider than what the save writes: the save sends an `UPDATE` only for a page holding a submitted column (`SqlRecordRepository.cs:1965-1989`, `PlanPageUpdates`), and only when such an `UPDATE` affects no row does it insert the key-only page row and repeat the `UPDATE`, as legacy repairs a missing page row (`Epi.Core/Data/Services/CollectedDataProvider.cs:1360-1380`; `SqlRecordRepository.cs:1447-1474`, `RunPageUpdateAsync`). A missing page row on a page with no submitted column stays missing. A denied write returns 403 before any DML, so nothing is written. A form whose page table the catalogue lacks never reaches the permission check: its metadata read refuses it with a 500, under "Insert on a form whose page table is missing".
  - Client: the form definition's flags are `null` until the server reports them, and only `false` restricts. Save is disabled on a stored record without `canUpdate` and on a new record without `canInsert`, so the first Save of a new record follows `canInsert` alone, and Delete is disabled without `canDelete` (`web/src/components/FormView.tsx:1134-1140`); New is disabled without `canInsert` (`web/src/components/RecordNavigator.tsx:137-138`). A save the flags deny, whether Save or leaving the record started it, sends no request and shows the permission banner (`web/src/state/useRecordEditor.ts:1103-1109`). A 403 from the server keeps the draft and shows the same banner. Its one action, Discard edits, drops the edits and keeps the record open, because the record still exists.
  - Proofs, final run of the run tree, 2026-11-26: `SqlPermissionReaderTests` 14 passed against the seeded login, among them `SeededLogin_GetsEveryPerTableFlag_AsHasPermsByNameReportsIt` (every per-table flag of both forms as `HAS_PERMS_BY_NAME` reports it) and `RepeatedCalls_OnOneReader_ReadTheCatalogueAndPermissionsAgain`. `PermissionTests` 37 passed through `FakeDataStore` with per-table denials, among them `EachWriteFollowsTheFlagThePermissionsDerive` (base UPDATE allowed and one page UPDATE denied: `PUT` 403 with no store update and `DELETE` 204; INSERT denied on any one table: `POST` 403), `UpdateNeedsInsertOnEveryPageWhoseRowIsMissing` (`PUT` 403 after the locked read, with nothing written, whether the submitted field is on the missing page or on the other), `UpdateRunsTheRequiredGateBeforeTheMissingPageInsertCheck` (the gate's 400 first, then the 403, each with nothing written), `TheStoreDecidesAnUpdateFromThePermissionsItHoldsWhenItRuns` (the store's own permissions decide, never the service's read) and `EachWriteReadsThePermissionsAgain`. `MissingPagePermissionSqlTests.UpdatePermissions_AreReadInsideTheUpdateTransactionBeforeItsFirstWrite` (its class renamed `WritePermissionSqlTests` after the run) passed against the rehearsal database: a `PUT` on a `FoodHistory` record lacking its `FoodHistory3` row, the `PUT` that repairs the row, and a `PUT` on the complete record each read the permissions of the base table and every page table inside their transaction, before its first write, while the service's own read runs outside it. After the run, the create and delete permission re-check ("Changed after the run") moved these proofs, checked on the changed tree, 2026-11-27, against a copy of the rehearsal database: `PermissionTests` 54 passed, among them `TheStoreDecidesEachWriteFromThePermissionsItHoldsWhenItRuns`, which replaces the update-only theory (18 cases: an insert, an update of a record lacking its second-page row and a soft delete, each decided by the permissions the fake store holds when it runs, against an argument granting everything or nothing, so an insert needs INSERT everywhere and no UPDATE, and a soft delete UPDATE on the base table alone), and `AGrantRevokedAfterTheServicesReadIsRefusedByTheStore` (a `POST`, `PUT` or `DELETE` whose grant is revoked after the service's read and before the store's own read is the action's 403 with no write call, no record added and the target unchanged); `WritePermissionSqlTests` 3 passed against the rehearsal database: `InsertPermissions_AreReadInsideTheInsertTransactionAfterTheFormLockBeforeItsFirstWrite` (a `POST` sends one permission statement inside its transaction, after `sys.sp_getapplock` and before its base `INSERT`, bound to `[FoodHistory]`, `[FoodHistory2]` and `[FoodHistory3]`), `DeletePermissions_AreReadInsideTheDeleteTransactionAfterTheLockedReadBeforeItsUpdate` (a `DELETE` sends one inside its transaction, after its `UPDLOCK` read and before its `UPDATE`, bound to `[FoodHistory]` alone) and `UpdatePermissions_AreReadInsideTheUpdateTransactionBeforeItsFirstWrite`, with the service's own read outside and before each transaction. `FormView.test.tsx` 156 passed, among them the permission case "canDelete true, canUpdate false", which finds Delete enabled and Save disabled, "enables the first Save of a new record and New by canInsert alone" and "keeps the draft and shows a permission banner when the server answers the $label with 403" (a `PUT` and a `POST`). `useRecordEditor.test.ts` 120 passed, among them the "permission flags (P2)" cases "refuses $write the flags deny without a request, and discards it in place" (an update without `canUpdate` and a create without `canInsert`, refused on leaving and on Save with no request) and "keeps the draft on a 403 to $write, and discards it in place". On the run's API (SC-1), both form definitions carry `canInsert`, `canUpdate` and `canDelete` true for the seeded login.
  - A real denied principal could not be tested, because it needs a new login or user, which the unchanged-database rule excludes; see "Denied principal (P2 permissions)" in "Parity gaps".
- **Permission reader (P2).** Legacy `GetPermissions` (`Epi.Data.SqlServer/SqlDatabase.cs:1804-1935`) first runs one catalogue query, `SELECT HAS_PERMS_BY_NAME(QUOTENAME(SCHEMA_NAME(schema_id)) + '.' + QUOTENAME(name), 'OBJECT', 'SELECT') AS have_select, name FROM sys.tables` (`:1809-1811`), and keeps only each row's `name`: the schema-qualified `have_select` flag decides nothing and is only debug-printed (`:1828-1838`). It then runs four loops over those names, for INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE and SELECT in turn, each sending one statement per table that concatenates the unquoted one-part name into `SELECT HAS_PERMS_BY_NAME('<table>', 'OBJECT', '<permission>')` (`:1847`, `:1870`, `:1894`, `:1918`), and reads only the value `1` as granted. The target (`src/EpiInfo.Data/Permissions/SqlPermissionReader.cs`) differs in three ways. It checks only the form's own base and page tables, as one catalogue read confirms them, not every table of the database. It reads only INSERT and UPDATE, for those tables together rather than one statement per table and permission, because soft delete is an `UPDATE` of the base row, so DELETE and SELECT decide nothing; a call therefore sends at most one catalogue read (none when the base and page table names are all blank) and one permission statement for up to 1,000 confirmed tables, plus one for each further 1,000 or part of 1,000 (none when the catalogue confirms no table). The re-check inside each write's transaction (`ReadResolvedAsync`), for the base table and every page table of an insert or an update and for the base table alone of a soft delete, sends the same permission statements on the transaction's connection for the tables that transaction already resolved, and no catalogue read. And each bracket-quoted table name travels only as the value of its own parameter (`@p0`, `@p1`, ...), never in the statement text. As in legacy, only `1` grants: `0` and `NULL` deny, and a table missing from the catalogue, base or page, gets no entry, so `FormPermissions` denies it. Nothing is cached: no call reuses an earlier call's catalogue or permission result, so a grant or revoke applies to the next call. Proof: `SqlPermissionReaderTests` (read-only, as the seeded login), `PermissionTests` (denials through `FakeDataStore`) and `MissingPagePermissionSqlTests` (the in-transaction read), 14, 37 and 1 passed in the final run of the run tree, 2026-11-26; after the run, with that class renamed `WritePermissionSqlTests` and covering the in-transaction read of every write, `PermissionTests` 54 and `WritePermissionSqlTests` 3 passed on the changed tree, 2026-11-27.
- **Metadata object roles.** Legacy reads whatever `metaFields.SourceTableName` names: `Project.GetTableData` sends a name without the `code` prefix to the collected-data provider instead of refusing it (`Epi.Core/Project.cs:1315-1325`). It also addresses whatever table `DataTableName` names (`Epi.Core/Data/Services/MetadataDbProvider.cs:2165-2192`). Metadata naming another form's table, or any readable table, would therefore have legacy show that table's rows as legal values, or read and write it as the form's records. The target gives each metadata reference one role (`src/EpiInfo.Data/Metadata/SqlMetadataReader.cs`, `MetadataQueries.cs`).
  - Code tables: a Legal Values field's options come only from a table of the shape legacy gives every code table. Its name starts with `code` in any letter case, the prefix Make View gives every code table (`Epi.Windows.MakeView/Dialogs/FieldDefinitionDialogs/CodeTableDefinition.cs:39-40`) and the one its code-table list filters on (`Epi.Data.SqlServer/SqlDatabase.cs:2065-2070`). Every column is a character column and none is an identity column, because legacy creates a code table only through `MetadataDbProvider.CreateCodeTable`, whose columns are all 255-character strings (`Epi.Core/Data/Services/MetadataDbProvider.cs:6083-6178, 6185-6199`); each of the 12 seeded `code*` tables holds exactly one `nvarchar(255)` column and no identity. It has no `GlobalRecordId` column, which every base and page table holds. Any other table gives an empty option list and none of its rows is read. The shape is read from the columns the existing catalogue read already returns, so options still cost at most two commands.
  - Page-table binding: a form is read and written only when its base table and a table for every one of its pages exist, the base table holding an identity `UniqueKey`, `RecStatus` and `GlobalRecordId`, and each page table `GlobalRecordId`. A page table's name is the form's `DataTableName` followed by its `metaPages.PageId`, an identity column that metadata DML cannot set, and legacy creates one page table for every page when it creates a form's tables (`Epi.Core/Data/Services/CollectedDataProvider.cs:2568-2638`). A missing page table therefore refuses the form, where legacy saves without that page's row ("Insert on a form whose page table is missing"). So swapping `DataTableName` between the seeded forms is refused: `CaseReport`'s page 1 would need `FoodHistory1`, and `FoodHistory`'s pages 2 and 3 would need `CaseReport2` and `CaseReport3`. A record-shaped table no form names is refused the same way, for want of a table per page id.
  - Claims by resolved identity: no name another form's metadata claims, each of its data-table names and each of them followed by each of its page ids, may resolve to one of the form's tables. One statement (`MetadataQueries.TableClaims`, with `@viewId` bound as `Int32` and the form's data-table name as `@baseName`, `String`) returns only the other forms' claims equal, under the catalogue read's case-insensitive collation, to one of the form's own names: it splits each own name before each of its last 1 to 11 code units, so the page claims `Food1` + `2` and `Food` + `12` of `Food12` are both found without pairing every name with every page id. The reader resolves each returned claim as it resolves the form's own names and compares the resulting table with the form's base and page tables by identity, one lookup per row. So distinct tables `Cases` and `cases` of a case-sensitive database each stay with the form that names it exactly, while any spelling that reaches one of the form's tables refuses the form. A form with several data-table names, which is itself refused, still claims each of them, so adding a name to another form never frees that form's tables.
  - Where the checks run: in every form metadata read, so a write repeats them inside its own transaction before any DML, and the permission check resolves only the tables of form metadata that passed them. A refused form answers 500 with the generic ProblemDetails body, which names no table. No seeded form or field is affected: `CaseReport` and `FoodHistory` name only their own record tables, each of their pages has its table, the claims statement returns no row for either, and every seeded Legal Values field names a `code` table of the legacy shape.
  - Remaining trust anchor: write access to the metadata. Moving `metaPages` rows between forms, or authoring a form whose metadata names an unclaimed record table and its page tables, needs write access to `metaPages`, `metaFields` or `metaViews`. A deployment therefore restricts writes to those tables to form designers, and grants the API's SQL login SELECT only on the `meta*` tables, the code tables and the forms' base and page tables, INSERT and UPDATE only on the base and page tables the save path writes, and no write access to `meta*`.
  - Proof: `SqlMetadataReaderObjectRoleTests` (46 cases built in memory, because no test may write a metadata row or a schema object): `ResolveRecordTables_BindsTheBaseAndEveryPageTableOrRefusesTheForm` (11, the seeded shapes bound, both `DataTableName` swaps, an orphaned record table, a missing or ambiguous page table and a non-record page or base table refused with fixed messages), `IsClaimedByAnotherForm_DecidesByTheTableEachClaimResolvesTo` (15, among them `Cases` and `cases` admitted both ways, `CASES`, `Food1` + `2`, `FOOD1` + `2` and `Food` + `12` refused), `IsClaimedByAnotherForm_DecidesALargeMalformedClaimSetInOnePass` (200,000 unrelated rows enumerated once and decided within its 5 s bound, the whole test taking 89 ms, where pairing each name with each page id would mean 10^10 candidates), `IsCodeTable_RequiresTheCodePrefixCharacterColumnsOnlyNoIdentityAndNoGlobalRecordId` (12, a `code` table with an `int` identity, `int` or `datetime` column refused), `IsRecordBaseTable_RequiresAnIdentityUniqueKeyRecStatusAndGlobalRecordId` (5) and `IsRecordPageTable_RequiresGlobalRecordId` (2). Read-only against the rehearsal database: `SqlMetadataReaderTests.ReadOptionsAsync_ColumnOutsideTheCodeTableRole_ReturnsNoOptionsAndReadsNoRow` (5: `CaseReport1.LastName`, `metaFields.Name`, `FoodHistory.GlobalRecordId`, `metaPages.Name` and `metaDbInfo.ProjectName` give no options and no code-table read) and `TableClaims_SeededForms_ReturnNoClaimAndTheFormsStillRead` (2: for each seeded form the statement returns no row, as a direct query pairing every other form's names with its page ids confirms, and the form reads with the statement bound as stated), with the seeded-form cases of `SqlMetadataReaderTests` unchanged, passed 2026-11-27.
- **Identifier quoting.** Legacy `InsertInEscape` adds `[` only when a name does not already start with it and `]` only when it does not already end with it, and never doubles an embedded `]` (`Epi.Data.SqlServer/SqlDatabase.cs:2094-2107`; `Epi.Core/Data/DbDriverBase.cs:472-487`). The target always wraps and always doubles `]`, so `a]b` becomes `[a]]b]` and `[x]` becomes `[[x]]]`, and it quotes only names the metadata reader returned (`src/EpiInfo.Data/Sql/SqlIdentifier.cs`). The two agree on every seeded name. No seeded name is a T-SQL reserved word, so the reserved-word write is proved on session temporary tables: `ReservedWordIdentifierTests.ReservedWordColumns_RoundTripThroughTheRepository` creates page columns `[Order]` (`nvarchar(4000)`), `[Select]` (`float`) and `[Group]` (`bit`), reads their schema through the reader's internal `tempdb` overload, and sends an insert, an update and a by-id read through the repository's internal open-connection overloads. Each recorded page `INSERT`, page `UPDATE` and record `SELECT` names the three columns in brackets and succeeded, and a direct read finds the values written. Unquoted, each of the three names is a syntax error: SQL Server 2022 rejects `SELECT Order`, `SELECT Select` and `SELECT Group`, and an `UPDATE ... SET` of each, with error 156, "Incorrect syntax near the keyword", and accepts the bracketed forms (a parse-only check under `SET PARSEONLY ON`, 2026-11-26, which ran no statement). The round trip passed in the final run of the run tree, 2026-11-26, among `ReservedWordIdentifierTests` 56 passed, with no table added to the rehearsal database. Proof: `SqlIdentifierTests` 26, `IdentifierProvenanceTests` 17, `RecordCommandIdentifierProvenanceTests` 2 (`tests/EpiInfo.Api.Tests/Persistence/SqlParameterizationTests.cs`) and `ReservedWordIdentifierTests` 56, passed in the final run of the run tree, 2026-11-26.
- **Logon stamp format (A11).** Legacy writes `WindowsIdentity.GetCurrent().Name`, in the form `DOMAIN\user` (`Epi.Core/Data/Services/CollectedDataProvider.cs:963-1004`). The target writes `Environment.UserName` of the API process, with no domain part. A process whose OS user has no name refuses each write that would store a stamp (an insert, an update, and a soft delete of an active record) with a 500 whose `writeOutcome` is `notWritten`, before any data statement, instead of stamping an invented name. A request that writes nothing keeps its usual answer: 404, 409, 400, 403, and the 204 of a `DELETE` of an already-deleted record.
- **EPIWEEK arguments.** Legacy `Rule_EPIWEEK` reads only its first two parameters (`Epi.Core.EnterInterpreter/Rules/Functions/Rule_EPIWEEK.cs:40-62`): a call with more than two arguments ignores the rest, and a call with none throws from `new DateTime(0, 1, 1)` (`:107, 124`). The target follows the `EPIWEEK(date[, firstDay])` signature and reports both as an `error` diagnostic, "EPIWEEK requires 1 or 2 arguments", skipping the statement. Legacy reads a text first day with `int.TryParse` in the current culture (`:51-62`); the target reads it with the invariant culture. A number first day counts only as an exact integer, so a non-integral number whose nearest double is whole, such as `2.0000000000000001`, keeps the default first day, Sunday, where legacy's `int.TryParse` of the double's text read the whole number (`src/EpiInfo.CheckCode/Functions/EpiWeekFunction.cs`). Proof: `EpiWeekFunctionTests` 87, passed in the final run of the run tree, 2026-11-26.
- **Check-code budgets.** Legacy reads `metaViews.CheckCode` whole, with no size limit (`Epi.Core/Data/Services/MetadataDbProvider.cs:462`), and interprets all of it when a view opens (`Epi.Enter.CheckCodeEngine/RunTimeView.cs:156`). The target reads and parses the program on every form definition and every field event, so it bounds each call. The store serves at most 1,048,576 UTF-16 code units, the 1 MiB request cap: the server sends a longer program's first 1,048,577 code units and none of the rest (`IDataStore.MaxCheckCodeLength`; `MetadataQueries.CheckCode`). The engine tests that length first, before it tests for a blank program, so a longer program is refused even when the first 1,048,577 code units, all the store sends, are white space. It parses at most 262,144 tokens, and a form definition or field event returns at most 100 diagnostics: when there are more, the first 99 are listed and one `LIMIT` warning, at the line of the first one left out, counts the rest, so none is dropped unreported and the API logs at most 100 lines per request (`src/EpiInfo.CheckCode/CheckCodeEngine.cs`, `MaxProgramLength`, `MaxTokens`, `MaxDiagnostics`). A program over the length or the token budget is one `LIMIT` warning at line 1 and runs nothing. A field event's run is also bounded by a work budget of 4,000,000 steps (`src/EpiInfo.CheckCode/Evaluation/Evaluator.cs`, `MaxWork`), because the evaluator's work is not linear in the tokens: a comparison reads both texts whole, a request value can be as long as the request, and a group name stands for all its members. The run is charged before the work is done: one step for each statement, expression node and name resolved; the text length of each comparison's operands, of a `NOT` operand, of a function's text arguments and of each value an `ASSIGN` stores, with an option's `metaFields.List` length; and each group expansion's member count. A group's members are resolved once per run (`EvaluationContext.GetGroupMembers`), where each mention used to rebuild them from all of the form's rows, and the statement counts that place static diagnostics are taken once per `IF` per run (`EventBlockNode.PreOrderSize`). A run that would pass the budget stops before that work with one `LIMIT` warning, "Check code exceeds the 4000000-step work limit; remaining statements skipped", at the line of the statement that ran out: the effects and diagnostics of the statements before it stand, as after a runtime error, and no later statement runs. The lexer, the parser and the evaluator stop on the request's cancellation, which the API answers as a client abort, with no response; the work meter polls it every 4,096 steps, so one long expression stops too. The seeded programs are far inside every budget: 443 and 2,428 code units, 36 and 258 tokens, at most one diagnostic each, and at most 18 work steps an event. Measured in a release build on the shared run host, 2026-11-27, as the minimum and median of seven calls that lex, parse and run: a program at the token budget of 131,052 `GOTO` warnings costs 81 and 144 ms to analyse, allocating 85 MiB, and 98 and 192 ms to run, 91 MiB; the most diagnostics both budgets allow, 209,695 one-token `QUIT` warnings, cost 122 and 173 ms to analyse, 118 MiB, and 139 and 183 ms to run, 127 MiB. The parser's diagnostics, at most one a token, are therefore bounded by the token and length budgets, and the parser is unchanged. Before the work budget, 20 comparisons of two 1,000,000-character texts that differ in case took 85 ms, so a stored program within both budgets, which can hold 65,000 comparisons, could run for minutes, and 2,000 mentions of a 1,000-member group took 556 ms and allocated 1.5 GB. Runs that fill the budget now end in their `LIMIT` warning within 0.15 s of evaluation: comparisons of 1,000,000-character texts stop after 40 ms for Thai text that differs in case, the slowest collation found at about 20 ns a code unit; 65,000 `DISABLE` statements naming a 1,000-member group stop after 133 ms, one `ENABLE` naming it 262,000 times after 58 ms. Programs bounded by the token budget alone stay within 0.2 s of evaluation: 65,000 runtime errors take 193 ms, and a 63-deep nest of 130,000 statements before a `GOTO` 58 ms. Proof: `DiagnosticsTests` (`ProgramAtABudget_IsParsedAndRuns`, `ProgramPastABudget_IsOneLimitWarningAndRunsNothing`, `ProgramPastTheLengthBudget_IsOneLimitWarningWhenWhiteSpaceLeadsIt`, `DiagnosticsAtTheBudget_AreAllListed`, `DiagnosticsPastTheBudget_EndInOneCountingLimitWarning`, `CancelledCall_ThrowsInsteadOfReporting`), `LexerTests` (`TryTokenize*`), `ParserTests.ParseStopsOnACancelledToken`, `EvaluatorTests` (`CancelledRun_StopsBeforeAnyStatement`, `CancellationInsideOneLongExpression_StopsItWithoutADiagnostic`, `ComparisonHeavyProgram_EndsInOneWorkLimitWarningQuickly_AndEarlierEffectsStand`, `GroupHeavyProgram_EndsInOneWorkLimitWarningQuickly_AndEarlierEffectsStand`, `GetGroupMembers_IsResolvedOncePerContext`, `ProgramAtTheWorkBudget_RunsFully_AndOneStepMoreEndsInOneLimitWarning`), `FormDefinitionCheckCodeTests` (`OversizedProgramIsOneLimitWarning`, `DiagnosticsPastTheBudgetAreListedAndLoggedUpToIt`), `FieldEventEndpointTests` (`OversizedStoredProgramIsOneLimitWarningWithNoEffects`, `DiagnosticsPastTheBudgetAreBoundedInTheResponseAndTheLog`, `WorkPastTheBudgetIsOneLimitWarningAfterTheEarlierEffects`), `CheckCodeScenarioTests`, unchanged, and `SqlMetadataReaderTests.GetCheckCodeAsync_ReturnsStoredProgram`, passed 2026-11-27.

## Legacy defects kept

The three scenarios with `legacyDefect: true` assert the legacy result, and the target reproduces it. Status: pass. In the final run of the run tree, 2026-11-26, `CheckCodeScenarioTests` 25 passed, among them `FieldEventScenario` `LY-02`, `LY-03` and `EC-AGE-03`, each against the program the API reads from the seeded database, and `LegacyDefectLedgerTests.LegacyDefectsListed` passed on this section.

### LY-02

- **Legacy behaviour:** `CaseReport` `Field Sex After` runs `IF Sex = "1" THEN ENABLE Pregnant ELSE DISABLE Pregnant CLEAR Pregnant END-IF` (check code lines 6-16, `testdata/sql/03_forms.sql:31-41`). In `codeSex`, `1` is Male and `2` is Female, so entering `Sex` "2" with `Pregnant` "1" disables and clears `Pregnant`.
- **Why it is a defect:** the condition is inverted. Pregnancy cannot be recorded for a female patient, and the field is enabled for a male one (`testdata/README.md`, finding 1).
- **Target:** kept. The scenario expects `Pregnant` with `enabled: false` and `value: null`, and the interpreter reproduces it. Status: pass (`FieldEventScenario` `LY-02` of `CheckCodeScenarioTests`, final run of the run tree, 2026-11-26).

### LY-03

- **Legacy behaviour:** the same rule runs in `Field LastName Before` (check code lines 18-28, `testdata/sql/03_forms.sql:43-53`). Entering `LastName` with `Sex` "2" and `Pregnant` "2" disables and clears `Pregnant`.
- **Why it is a defect:** the same inverted condition, reached from a second event, so a female patient's pregnancy answer is cleared whenever the user enters `LastName`.
- **Target:** kept. The scenario expects `Pregnant` with `enabled: false` and `value: null`, and the interpreter reproduces it. Status: pass (`FieldEventScenario` `LY-03` of `CheckCodeScenarioTests`, final run of the run tree, 2026-11-26).

### EC-AGE-03

- **Legacy behaviour:** `FoodHistory` `Field DOB After` runs `ASSIGN AGE = YEARS(DOB, DateofInterview)` (check code line 6, `testdata/sql/03_forms.sql:60`). `YEARS` computes `new DateTime((later - earlier).Ticks).Year - 1` (`Epi.Core.EnterInterpreter/Rules/Functions/Rule_Years.cs:55-66`), turning the elapsed days into a year count from year 1. For `DOB` 2000-02-29 and `DateofInterview` 2011-02-28 it returns 11.
- **Why it is a defect:** it is not a birthday-based age. The span is 4,017 days, exactly the length of calendar years 1 to 11, so `new DateTime` lands on 0012-01-01 and the result is 11 even though the eleventh birthday has not been reached; a birthday-based age gives 10 (`testdata/README.md`, finding 2).
- **Target:** kept, as an exact port: `YearsFunction` (`src/EpiInfo.CheckCode/Functions/YearsFunction.cs`) ports legacy `YEARS`, `new DateTime((later - earlier).Ticks).Year - 1`, negated when the first date is later, and `null` unless both arguments are dates. Legacy `YEARS` matches all 356 stored sample ages (`testdata/README.md`, finding 2), and the scenario expects `Age` 11. Status: pass. In the final run of the run tree, 2026-11-26, `YearsFunctionTests` 38 passed, among them `LeapDayBirthDateKeepsLegacyResultOfEleven` (2000-02-29 to 2011-02-28 gives 11), and `FieldEventScenario` `EC-AGE-03` of `CheckCodeScenarioTests` passed.

## Search semantics

`POST .../records/search` follows legacy Find, because the request's tie-break makes legacy semantics win (C-4). The records-list `q` keeps the request's case-insensitive "contains", because it has no legacy counterpart.

### Legacy extraction

- **Dialog**: at most six fields can be selected (`Epi.Windows.Enter/Dialogs/FindRecordDialog.cs:293-317`), and each control's text is trimmed (`:355-374`). A value splits on exactly `" or "`, `" OR "`, `" Or "` or `" oR "`, dropping empty entries. A leading `<=`, `>=`, `<>`, `>` or `<` is read as the operator, `=` by default, and stripped from the value (`:708-770`). Criteria on different fields combine with AND, and the alternatives of one field with OR.
- **Query**: the base table is `INNER JOIN`ed only to the pages that hold a criterion field (`Epi.Core/Data/Services/CollectedDataProvider.cs:1940-1967`). A system-field criterion maps to an unbound page with no table name, and the join then fails (the `GlobalRecordId` and `RecStatus` rows below). Predicates are built per type, as in the table below, and `RecStatus > 0` is appended (`:2249`). The open record is removed after the query only when the caller passes a non-null `currentView` with the searched view's name. The standard Enter Find command passes none (`Epi.Windows.Enter/Forms/EnterMainForm.cs:406-409`; `Epi.Windows.Enter/Dialogs/FindRecordDialog.cs:67, 80-81, 676`), so a matching open record stays in its result. Only the linked-record viewer passes one (`Epi.Windows.Enter/Forms/LinkedRecordsViewer.cs:292, 336`), and the provider then removes that record (`Epi.Core/Data/Services/CollectedDataProvider.cs:2256-2264`).
- **Hydration**: the matching base rows are re-read and each page is joined in memory; a base row without a row on some page is dropped (`:2289-2298, 2357-2375`). The result is therefore the matching base rows that have a row on every page, in no stated order.

| Field kind | Legacy | Target |
|------------|--------|--------|
| `text`, `multiline`, `legalValues`, except Phone Number | Blank text is rejected by the dialog (`FindRecordDialog.cs:611-643`). The operator prefix is stripped and ignored; a value left empty by a bare prefix such as `>` becomes `IS NULL` (`CollectedDataProvider.cs:2186-2193`). A value containing `%`, `*` or `?` becomes `LIKE` on an inlined literal, with `*` → `%` and single quotes removed (`:2195-2206`). Multiline compares `SUBSTRING(col, 1, 255) = SUBSTRING(@p, 1, 255)` (`:2212-2222`); anything else is `= @p` (`:2224-2231`) | The same, with every value a parameter: blank → 400; a bare prefix → `IS NULL`; the `LIKE` pattern keeps its quotes; `_` and `[...]` keep their SQL meaning and `?` matches literally, as in legacy. The multiline 255-character comparison's parameter is Unicode `nvarchar(max)`, where legacy bound `DbType.AnsiString` (`CollectedDataProvider.cs:2212-2222`); the effect is under "Parameterisation" in "Target choices" below |
| Phone Number (`fieldType` `text`, chosen by the `metaFieldTypes` name) | Blank rejected by the dialog. A bare prefix gives `IS NULL`; otherwise `col <prefix> '<value>'`, a string comparison with no wildcard path (`CollectedDataProvider.cs:2118-2131`) | The same, parameterised |
| `number` | Blank → `IS NULL`. Each alternative must parse as a number once the prefix is stripped, so a bare prefix is rejected (`FindRecordDialog.cs:418-463`). `col <prefix> <number>` (`CollectedDataProvider.cs:2046-2066`) | The same, with an invariant number parameter typed by the column. Legacy parsed each alternative with `Double.Parse` in the current culture (`FindRecordDialog.cs:449-454`) and wrote that double's `ToString()`, at most 15 significant digits on .NET Framework 4.8, into the statement (`CollectedDataProvider.cs:2058-2063`), so it compared a rounded number and could not tell apart integers a double does not hold, such as 9007199254740992 and 9007199254740993. The target compares the criterion exactly against integer columns, never converting the column to `float`: an exact integer is bound as that `bigint`; any other number as the exact `decimal` half-way inside the integer interval that holds it (`9007199254740992.5` for `9007199254740992.5`, 2.5 for `2.0000000000000001`), or just beyond the `bigint` range on its side. On a `float` column the number is bound as its nearest `float`. Unparseable or a bare prefix → 400 |
| `option` | Treated as numeric by the dialog (`FindRecordDialog.cs:418`). Blank passes `null` to the provider, which fails with a null dereference (`CollectedDataProvider.cs:2186`), so legacy reports an error and returns no rows. Otherwise `= @p` as text, prefix ignored (`:2224-2231`) | Blank → 400. Otherwise an integer index, prefix ignored, `= @p` |
| `date` | Blank → `IS NULL`. Each alternative is parsed with the current culture, and a bare prefix is rejected (`FindRecordDialog.cs:464-510`). `col <prefix> @p` as `DbType.DateTime` (`CollectedDataProvider.cs:2135-2169`) | Blank → `IS NULL`. Only `YYYY-MM-DD` is accepted, bound as `datetime`; a date outside 1753-01-01 to 9999-12-31, another format or a bare prefix → 400 |
| `checkbox` | Blank rejected (`FindRecordDialog.cs:511-517`). Each alternative, prefix included, must equal the configured Yes or No text (defaults `Yes` and `No`, `Epi.Core/Configuration_Static.cs:710-711`) or `(+)` or `(-)` (`FindRecordDialog.cs:518-554`). `= @p` as `DbType.Boolean` (`CollectedDataProvider.cs:2022-2045`) | Blank → 400. The strings `Yes` and `(+)` → `= 1`, `No` and `(-)` → `= 0`, case-sensitive, using the defaults because the new app has no legacy configuration file. A JSON boolean is also accepted. Any other string, `true`, `1` and `0` included → 400 |
| `GlobalRecordId` (system) | Offered by the dialog (`FindRecordDialog.cs:116-120`), whose generic branch rejects a blank. The provider would build `IS NULL` for a bare prefix, otherwise `basetable.GlobalRecordId <prefix> '<value>'` (`CollectedDataProvider.cs:2068-2082`). The search fails before any result: `GlobalRecordIdField` is a `PredefinedDataField`, not a `RenderableField` (`Epi.Core/Services/Fields/GlobalRecordIdField.cs:13`; `Epi.Core/Services/Fields/PredefinedDataField.cs:13`), so no page's `Fields` holds it (`Epi.Core/Page.cs:364-386`). `GetTableName` returns a new, unbound `Page` (`CollectedDataProvider.cs:579-591`; `Page.cs:147-149`) whose `TableName` is `null` (`:219-231`). The unconditional page join (`CollectedDataProvider.cs:1940-1967`) then emits `INNER JOIN [] pageTable0` (`Epi.Core/Util.cs:1193-1196`), and SQL Server rejects the empty identifier | A base-table criterion: the predicate is on the base-table column `GlobalRecordId`, so the field adds no criterion-specific page join and the legacy unbound-page join failure is not reproduced. The search still joins every page table ("Result set" in "Target choices" below), so only base rows with a row on every page are eligible. Blank → 400; a bare prefix → `IS NULL`; otherwise the prefix comparison on the base column, parameterised |
| `RecStatus` (system) | Offered by the dialog (`FindRecordDialog.cs:116-120`) and handled by its generic branch. The provider would build `= @p` as text, then append `RecStatus > 0` (`CollectedDataProvider.cs:2224-2231, 2249`). The search fails before any result, as for `GlobalRecordId`: `RecStatusField` is also a `PredefinedDataField` (`Epi.Core/Services/Fields/RecStatusField.cs:14`), so its criterion maps to the same unbound page and the join fails | A base-table criterion: the predicate is on the base-table column `RecStatus`, so the field adds no criterion-specific page join and the legacy unbound-page join failure is not reproduced. The search still joins every page table ("Result set" in "Target choices" below), so only base rows with a row on every page are eligible. It is matched as an integer, prefix ignored, `= @p` on the base column, inside the selected set |
| `unsupported`, `label`, `group`, `UniqueKey`, unknown | The dialog lists no labels, groups, mirrors, relates, grids or command buttons (`FindRecordDialog.cs:103-114`) | 400. Unsupported types, Yes/No included, are not searchable |

### Target choices

The one list of Find departures and retentions (C-4, C-18):

- **Kept**: the six-field limit, so a seventh criterion field is a 400 on `criteria`; trimming of each string value; the split and prefix rules; AND across fields and OR within one. A field named twice is a 400, since the dialog cannot select a field twice. A list of more than six criteria whose items all pass the search body's member check, which answers a longer list holding an item of the wrong JSON kind with its own one message `criteria must be an array of at most 6 {field, value} objects.`, can only name a seventh field, repeat one or hold an entry that names none, so it is answered with the one message `At most 6 fields can be searched at once.` under `criteria` before any entry is read, whatever its entries name: 100,000 `null` entries, a 600,014-byte body, give a 302-byte 400 in 0.03 to 0.05 s; measured 2026-11-27 against the parser without the list check, the same body gave a 4,889,148-byte 400 holding one indexed message per entry (`SearchCriteriaParser.Parse`; `SearchCriteriaParserTests`, `ListAndSearchBoundsTests`).
- **Value bound**: kept from legacy. Each string value holds at most 32,767 UTF-16 code units (`RecordQueryRules.MaxSearchValueLength`), counted as received, before trimming; one more is the 400 `<field> search values can hold at most 32767 characters.` under the field's canonical name, checked once the field resolves and before the value is split, so no alternative or pattern of it is built. 32,767 is what one legacy Find text box holds: the dialog creates each value box as a `MaskedTextBox` or `TextBox` without setting `MaxLength` (`Epi.Windows.Enter/Dialogs/FindRecordDialog.cs:139-177, 203-217`), so each takes the Windows Forms default of 32,767 characters, and it shows one box for each of up to six fields (`:293-317`). So one value holds at most 6,554 alternatives, one character each with `" or "` between them ((32,767 + 4) / 5, rounded down), and one search at most 6 × 6,554 = 39,324 alternatives and 6 × 32,767 = 196,602 code units of value text, the most equality and `IS NULL` alternatives one search holds. A plain multiline value is compared on its first 255 characters by legacy and the target alike (`Epi.Core/Data/Services/CollectedDataProvider.cs:2208-2223`; `SUBSTRING(col, 1, 255)` in `SearchSqlBuilder`), so two 17,000-unit multiline values, each in a box of its own, cost what two short ones do.
- **Search-wide bounds**: a design reconciliation of the dialog's six boxes with the 30-second command timeout (0.9.2); legacy checked neither. Over the conditions that parsed, one search holds at most 6,554 alternatives other than equality and `IS NULL` (`RecordQueryRules.MaxRowComparedAlternatives`, one box at one character per alternative): every wildcard pattern, comparison (`<>`, `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=`) and multiline 255-character comparison counts, and one more is the one 400 `One search can hold at most 6554 wildcard, comparison and multiline alternatives in all.` under `criteria`. And it holds at most 32,767 code units of wildcard pattern text (`RecordQueryRules.MaxSearchPatternTextLength`, one box), each pattern counted as it runs, after its prefix is stripped, `*` becomes `%` and, past 4,000 units, its runs of `%` collapse; one more is the one 400 `The wildcard patterns of one search can hold at most 32767 characters in all.` under `criteria`. A search past both holds both messages; one value within its own bound passes both alone, so a 400 holds at most six messages. Why these alternatives and not the others: a Find statement's work per row is the alternatives it compares row by row times the text of each value it reads. A `LIKE`, comparison or prefix alternative has no set-based form over page tables that carry no index on their data columns and whose schema stays unchanged, and a pattern longer than 4,000 units compiles into a long-pattern program at about 0.27 ms of cold compilation per code unit. An equality does: packed, its list is matched by one merge or hash semi-join, and scalar it is one comparison; and a condition's `IS NULL` alternatives are one `IS NULL` term. So equality and `IS NULL` count toward neither bound and are bounded only by their values. Measured on the rehearsal SQL Server 2022 with no bound but the field limit: 80,000 `%a<i>%` alternatives on `CaseReport1.LastName` (948,936 bytes) and 60,000 on `FoodHistory2.PatientAddress` ran 30.1 s and failed as a 503 command timeout, 20,000 took 16 s, six fields each holding one 32,767-unit `%_…%` pattern failed as a 503 after 30 s, and five fields made to scan 3,080 packed patterns took 8.6 s. Measured 2026-11-27 through the API with the bounds, on a copy of the rehearsal database holding the records of earlier test runs (1,448 active `CaseReport` records, 21 of them with a 4,000-character `LastName`, 86,890 `LastName` characters in all, where the freshly restored rehearsal data holds 1,766), two consecutive runs each: the costliest shapes at the bounds ran in 9.3 to 9.5 s (6,554 four-unit `%q<x>%` patterns, each `x` a different character, spread over five `CaseReport` text fields, each condition ending in `_%` so that it scans its whole packed list), 8.4 to 8.5 s (the same over six fields), 6.2 to 6.3 s (over five `FoodHistory` text fields, an `ntext` field on each page among them), 7.7 to 7.9 s (one 32,767-unit `%_…` pattern on `CaseReport1.LastName`), 9.0 s cold and 1.6 s warm (on the `ntext` `FoodHistory2.PatientAddress`), 1.6 s (on the `ntext` `FoodHistory3.BuyLastTenDays`) and 5.0 s (six patterns sharing the 32,767 units on `CaseReport`); the searches the bounds leave to their values answered 200 in at most 0.16 s: two plain 17,000-unit values on `PatientAddress` and `BuyLastTenDays`, two of 32,767, six `CaseReport` fields of 6,000 distinct equality alternatives each, and six of 6,554 one-character equality alternatives (39,324 in all); one value of 32,768 units, 6,555 such patterns, 32,768 units of patterns over two fields and seven criteria were each the one 400 for their bound in at most 0.02 s with a body of 302 to 349 bytes; and each QA search above is a 400 under its field for the value bound in at most 0.02 s, six fields of 13,000 alternatives with one message for each field (665 bytes). The bounds cap the alternatives and the pattern text, not the work: a `LIKE` alternative with a leading wildcard costs time per character of every value it scans, so a search's cost still grows with the rows and the text they hold. On the same copy one `LastName` criterion within every bound, 5,461 `%q` patterns (32,764 units), took 23.8 to 24.1 s, 4,681 `%q%` patterns 20.6 to 20.7 s and 4,096 distinct `%q<x>%` patterns 17.9 to 18.1 s, against 0.21 s for the 4,681 on the freshly restored data. The Find admission ("Find admission" in "SQL Server limits") bounds how many such statements run at once, and the command timeout bounds each. `SearchCriteriaParserTests` pins the three bounds, `ListAndSearchBoundsTests` their 400s over HTTP and the 200 of the two 17,000-unit multiline values, and `SearchStatementBoundsTests` (`Search_CostliestCriteriaAtTheBounds_RunWithinTheDefaultCommandTimeout`) runs the costliest shapes at the bounds through the repository on the rehearsal database within the default command timeout.
- **Result set**: one statement `INNER JOIN`s the base table to every page table and applies the criteria. For the legacy searches that reach hydration, the all-page join reproduces legacy's final, hydrated result (base rows with every page row), though not legacy's two-step query. A legacy search on a system field fails at its join first (the `GlobalRecordId` and `RecStatus` rows above).
- **Set filter**: `RecStatus = 1`, or every row with `includeDeleted=true`, replacing `RecStatus > 0` (C-16).
- **Open record**: excluded on the server through `excludeGlobalRecordId` (C-19), before `total` and the cap. The id is matched exactly as a lookup by id matches it, so a spelling that differs from the stored id only by trailing spaces excludes nothing. This is a behavioural difference from standard Enter Find, which keeps a matching open record in its result, not only a change of timing. Legacy removed the open record only on the linked-record path, after the query ("Query" above).
- **Order and cap**: results are ordered by `UniqueKey` and capped at 200; `total` counts all matches and `truncated` is `total > 200`. Each item's `position` is its `ROW_NUMBER()` over the selected set by `UniqueKey`, computed before the criteria filter, so it equals the navigation position. Legacy had no order and no cap.
- **Parameterisation**: every value is a parameter, so quotes stay in `LIKE` patterns where legacy removed them. Text-family values are bound as Unicode `nvarchar(max)`, so a criterion reaches the comparison with the characters entered. Legacy did not keep them for every comparison: it bound the multiline 255-character comparison as `DbType.AnsiString` (`CollectedDataProvider.cs:2212-2222`), which its SQL Server driver sends as `varchar` (`Epi.Data.SqlServer/SqlDatabase.cs:525-526`), and it inlined the `LIKE` pattern (`CollectedDataProvider.cs:2205`) and the Phone Number value (`:2130`) as literals without the `N` prefix. Each reached the comparison in the code page of the database collation, 1252 for `SQL_Latin1_General_CP1_CI_AS`, with a character outside it best-fit mapped or replaced by `?` (on this server `CONVERT(varchar(50), N'Łódź 漢')` gives `Lódz ?`). Such a criterion could miss a stored value holding the original characters, or match one holding the mapped text; the target matches the characters entered. Legacy's plain equality already bound `DbType.String` (`:2231`), so it is unchanged.
- **Departures in the table**: number criteria compared exactly against integer columns, where legacy compared the criterion's rounded double text; ISO dates bound as `datetime`; a blank Option rejected cleanly instead of the legacy null dereference; JSON booleans accepted for checkboxes beside `Yes`/`(+)` → 1 and `No`/`(-)` → 0, case-sensitive; the system fields `GlobalRecordId` and `RecStatus` searched as base-table criteria, where the legacy join on their unbound page fails before any result; Yes/No fields not searchable.
- **Proof**: pass, final run, 2026-11-26, on the run tree, `dotnet test EpiInfo.Web.sln` on the freshly loaded rehearsal database. `SearchCriteriaParserTests` 241 passed (the coercions, the split and prefix rules, the six-field limit) and `RecordQueryRulesTests` 124 passed. `ListAndSearchTests` 26 passed (returned rows on records the tests create, exclusion before `total` and the cap, positions in both sets), with the other classes of `tests/EpiInfo.Api.Tests/Records/ListAndSearchTests.cs`: `ListAndSearchBoundsTests` 15 (among them `SearchArrays_PublishTheirBounds`, which reads the six-criterion and 200-item bounds from the served OpenAPI document), `SearchRequestBindingTests` 2, `FakeDataStoreFindTests` 107, `FakeDataStoreTrailingBlankFindTests` 12, `GridFilterTextSqlTests` 53, `FindPaddingSqlTests` 19 and `RequestParameterCoercionTests` 213, all passed. `SearchStatementBoundsTests` had one failure ("SQL Server limits" below).

### Grid q

The records-list `q` is a case-insensitive "contains" over the grid columns. It converts each column to `nvarchar(max)` (style 23 for `datetime`) and compares under `Latin1_General_100_CI_AS` with `LIKE @q ESCAPE '\'`, with `%`, `_`, `[` and `\` escaped in `@q`, so it does not depend on the database collation. A blank `q` applies no filter. A `q` of any length is that literal contains, never a 400. When its escaped and `%`-wrapped pattern is longer than SQL Server's 4,000-unit `LIKE` limit, that is a `q` longer than 3,998 characters counting each `%`, `_`, `[` and `\` as two, the one list statement instead runs the long-pattern program ("Wildcard patterns", below) over the same converted column texts under the same collation. There `q` is one literal run between two `%`: its seek finds candidates with `PATINDEX` on prefixes of `q`, written with `%`, `_` and `[` as the one-member sets `[%]`, `[_]` and `[[]`, because `PATINDEX` takes no `ESCAPE` clause, and each candidate compares the whole `q` with `=`, which needs no escaping. So a long `q` lists the records the one `LIKE` that SQL Server refuses to run would list, where `Latin1_General_100_CI_AS` matches an expansion (`æ` and `ae`, `ß` and `ss`) or ignores a character (`NUL`, the soft hyphen) included: `GridQ_PastTheLikePatternLimit_KeepsTheCollationsExpansions` (`tests/EpiInfo.Api.Tests/Records/ListAndSearchTests.cs`) lists a record storing `æ` for a 4,001-character `q` holding `ae`, and the reverse, and `SearchStatementBoundsTests` compares the long and the native filter on seeded texts holding `fi` under filters carrying `ﬁ`, `NUL`, the soft hyphen and `😀`. The one bound left, that the seek looks for 33 prefixes of `q`, is recorded under "Parity gaps" ("Find wildcard patterns longer than 4,000 code units"). `q` has no legacy counterpart.

### SQL Server limits

Two SQL Server limits bear on a Find search: a command binds at most 2,100 parameters, of which `sp_executesql` takes two, and an `nvarchar` pattern of `LIKE`, `PATINDEX`, `CHARINDEX` or `REPLACE` holds at most 8,000 bytes, 4,000 UTF-16 code units, beyond which each fails with error 8152, while `=` has no such limit. Measured on the rehearsal SQL Server 2022, an `sp_executesql` call with 2,098 statement parameters runs and one with 2,099 fails with the 2,100-parameter error.

- **Alternatives**: a search holds as many alternatives as its values admit, at most 6,554 in one value and 39,324 in six ("Value bound" in "Target choices"), of which at most 6,554 are other than equality and `IS NULL` ("Search-wide bounds"), where legacy took any number. While it holds at most 2,000 alternatives other than `IS NULL` across its fields, the Find statement binds one parameter per such alternative, plus at most three (the selected-set `RecStatus`, the `TOP` cap and the excluded id), so at most 2,003 parameters. With more, every condition is bound packed: its `IS NULL` alternatives become one `IS NULL` term, and its other values, grouped by operator and by the type their scalar parameters bind (a number on an integer column binds `bigint` or `decimal`), one `nvarchar(max)` parameter each holding a packed list: ASCII text giving the element count and the cumulative offsets of the element tokens, ten digits each, followed by the tokens. It is matched by `EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM (<the list decoded, one row per element>) AS [d] WHERE <the operator's predicate on [d].[v]>)`: cross-joined `VALUES` digit tables, as many as the count has digits, number the elements, and `SUBSTRING` reads each token at the offsets the list gives, so the count, the offsets and the values all stay in the parameter. The decoded set is ordered by value under `TOP (2147483647)`, which makes SQL Server decode each element once and lets it match the set with one merge or hash semi-join, or scan it from one spool for the other operators, rather than decode the list again for every row. A packed statement alone also ends with `OPTION (USE HINT ('DISABLE_OPTIMIZER_ROWGOAL', 'FORCE_LEGACY_CARDINALITY_ESTIMATION'))`: SQL Server costs the decoding as almost free, so under the row goal of `TOP`, which buys nothing because `COUNT_BIG(*) OVER ()` reads every match first, or under the cardinality estimator of level 130 and later, it can still plan it once per candidate row, and the hints make every level plan it once per list as levels 100 and 110 do without a row goal. Measured 2026-11-26 through the builder on `CaseReport`, one `GlobalRecordId` equality and 5,000 `LIKE` alternatives exceed the 30-second command timeout unhinted at levels 100 and 160, and at 160 with the row-goal hint alone, and run in 0.9 s with both; a `CaseReport1.Address` search of one equality and 5,000 patterns, which the row-goal hint alone takes from 5.6 s past the timeout at 160, runs in 3.7 s. A condition then binds at most twelve parameters, and one more when it holds patterns longer than 4,000 units, which are never packed ("Wildcard patterns"), so a six-field search stays far under the limit. Each element is built as the scalar parameter it replaces and decodes to exactly that parameter's SQL type and value: text through the hexadecimal of its UTF-16LE code units, read back as `nvarchar(max)`, or for a `LIKE` pattern as the `nvarchar(4000)` that `LIKE` converts its `nvarchar(max)` parameter to, which keeps every code unit and compares under the column's collation as a parameter does; integers, bits and dates as text read back into their own type; an integer column's `decimal` criterion as its text read back as `decimal(38, s)`, `s` the largest scale of its list; floats as the sign, exponent and fraction of their IEEE 754 bits at fixed widths, rebuilt by exact arithmetic, because SQL Server's text-to-float parse is not correctly rounded. Packed and per-alternative statements therefore return the same rows, which `SearchStatementBoundsTests` (`tests/EpiInfo.Data.Tests/Records/SqlRecordRepositoryTests.cs`) compares on both forms, also under non-default session `SET` options. The decoding uses no `OPENJSON`, `JSON_VALUE`, `STRING_SPLIT` or `xml` method, so it runs at every database compatibility level (Compatibility level, above). Final run, 2026-11-26, on the run tree, on the Compose `sqlserver` service with its default parallelism settings (max degree of parallelism 0, cost threshold for parallelism 5): `SearchStatementBoundsTests` 20 passed and 1 failed. The failure, `Search_PackedEqualityAndLikeOnGlobalRecordId_DecodesOnceWithinTheDefaultTimeout`, is the packed statement of two `GlobalRecordId` equalities and 9,998 `LIKE` alternatives on `CaseReport`, which exceeded the 30-second command timeout after 31.3 s (`SqlException`, "Execution Timeout Expired"); SC-1 fails on it ("Run results"). The rerun without a database reset gave the same 20 passes and the same one failure, after 31.0 s; `Search_AlternativesAcrossTheScalarBudget_ReturnTheUnionOfChunkedScalarSearches`, whose three sizes failed in the rerun of tree `d450f367`, passed in both runs. With max degree of parallelism 1 and cost threshold for parallelism 50 on the same server, after a fresh load, every `SearchStatementBoundsTests` case passed, the packed case in 3.1 to 3.7 s.
- **Legacy alternatives**: legacy inlined each wildcard pattern and each number into the statement text and bound one parameter for every other alternative other than `IS NULL` (`Epi.Core/Data/Services/CollectedDataProvider.cs:2046-2066, 2195-2231`). It checked no limit, so a search with more than about 2,098 parameter-bound alternatives (text and multiline values without a wildcard, Phone Number, `GlobalRecordId`, dates, checkboxes, options) failed in SQL Server and returned no result, while it ran any number of wildcard and number alternatives: measured, a legacy-shaped statement with 2,106 inlined wildcard alternatives on `CaseReport1.LastName` runs. The target has no such failure: it binds every alternative it accepts, at most 39,324 within its bounds, without reaching the parameter limit, and its packed and per-alternative statements return the same rows (`SearchStatementBoundsTests`). A packed statement still runs under the 30-second command timeout, which the builder's packed `GlobalRecordId` case of 10,000 alternatives, 9,998 of them `LIKE` patterns, more than one value or the search-wide bound admits from a client, exceeds under the server's default parallelism settings ("Alternatives" above). It returns legacy's matches only for alternatives it reads as legacy does, under the set filter, open record, and order and cap of "Target choices" above: a wildcard pattern holding a `[...]` set longer than 4,000 code units that is unclosed or that no cut keeping its members divides is a 400 ("Wildcard patterns" below, and "Find wildcard patterns longer than 4,000 code units" in "Parity gaps"); number, date, option, checkbox and system-field criteria are read as "Departures in the table" lists; and text criteria are compared with the characters entered, where legacy's `varchar` binding and literals could map them ("Parameterisation").
- **Wildcard patterns**: the target binds a Find `LIKE` pattern as an `nvarchar` parameter, of which SQL Server takes at most 4,000 UTF-16 code units (`RecordQueryRules.MaxLikePatternLength`). Measured: an `nvarchar(max)` pattern of 4,001 `%` fails with error 8152, "String or binary data would be truncated", for the Find `col LIKE @p` and the grid `q` statements alike. A pattern within the limit is sent exactly as written, as `col LIKE @p`. A longer one that holds no `[` has each run of `%`, after `*` → `%`, collapsed to one `%`, which matches exactly the same text: with no `ESCAPE` clause every `%` outside a `[...]` set is a wildcard, and a run of them matches what one does. Inside a set a `%` is a member (`[a-%%]%%` and `[a-%]%` match different text), so a pattern holding `[` is never rewritten. A pattern still longer than 4,000 units is matched whole, with no 400 (`src/EpiInfo.Data/Records/SearchSqlBuilder.cs`, "Long patterns"): its condition's long patterns are compiled into one program, bound as one ASCII parameter, which a recursive CTE runs over the column's values in the same single `SELECT`, ended by `OPTION (MAXRECURSION 0)`, so neither the statement text nor its parameter count grows with the pattern, and a condition holding one is never packed. SQL Server matches a literal run of a pattern by the collation's equality, under which one character can rank equal to several (`æ` to `ae`, `ß` to `ss`, `ﬁ` to `fi`) and an ignorable one (`NUL`; the soft hyphen under `Latin1_General_100_CI_AS`; every supplementary character under `SQL_Latin1_General_CP1_CI_AS`) to none, while `_` and a set match exactly one character of the collation and an expansion never straddles a literal and a wildcard (measured: `æ` is `LIKE 'ae'` but not `LIKE 'a_'`, and `xæy` is `LIKE 'x_y'`). So the program reads the pattern as atoms, each a maximal literal run, a `_`, a whole `[...]` set or a run of `%`, and never cuts a literal run between two comparisons. Its steps are windows of whole atoms of at most 3,998 units, compared with `LIKE`; a literal run longer than that, compared with `=`, which has no length limit, with `x` appended to both sides because `=` ignores trailing blanks that `LIKE` keeps; and a set longer than that, cut into pieces. Each step tracks where the text matched so far really ends: a window first tries its own character count, then bisects for the shortest prefix of the rest that `LIKE W + '%'` accepts, which is monotone in the prefix length, extends it to a character boundary of the collation (where the two code units around it are not one `_` and neither is ignorable) and accepts it when it is `LIKE W`; a run step bisects for the first prefix not below the run and accepts the first equal one at a boundary. The segments between runs of `%` are placed in order: the first from the start of the value; each middle one at the leftmost candidate `PATINDEX` finds for its first atoms, retried after a candidate whose steps fail; the last, when it fits a 3,999-unit `LIKE '%' + W`, by that `LIKE`, else, when each of its literal runs fits a window, matched backwards from the end of the value, and otherwise placed as a middle one before an end that only ignorable characters may fill, as a pattern with no `%` ends. A segment that starts with a literal run seeks the leftmost hit of 33 of its prefixes, cut at text-element boundaries, the longest within 3,998 units: a value character the collation expands can straddle the end of one prefix, which then misses the match, but not the end of each of 33 consecutive ones. A set longer than a window is cut into sets of at most 3,998 units, each `[`, members and `]`, only between two members where neither is `-` or a zero-width joiner, the second is neither `^` nor a combining mark and the two are not one surrogate pair (`LikePatternSyntax.TrySplitSet`, `src/EpiInfo.Domain/Search/SearchCondition.cs`), so no range, negation or combining sequence changes; a value's character then matches when it is `LIKE` some piece, or for a negated set when it is one `_` and `LIKE` none. Measured on SQL Server 2022 for every BMP character and multi-unit strings, under `Latin1_General_100_CI_AS`, `SQL_Latin1_General_CP1_CI_AS`, `Latin1_General_100_BIN2`, `Latin1_General_100_CS_AS_SC` and `Czech_CI_AS`, the pieces of each cut set match exactly what the whole set matches. Every comparison runs under the column's collation, on pattern texts read as `nvarchar(4000)`, and counts characters as that collation does, a surrogate pair as one under an `_SC` collation. `SearchStatementBoundsTests` (`tests/EpiInfo.Data.Tests/Records/SqlRecordRepositoryTests.cs`) shows the program returning exactly the rows of native `LIKE`, with the window limit forced to 0, 3 and 8 so that run steps, set pieces, bisections and extensions all run: for a corpus of literals, `_`, sets (`[]`, `[^]`, an unclosed `[a`, ranges, `[a-c-e]`), runs of `%` and several segments, on `CaseReport1.LastName` and the `ntext` `FoodHistory3.BuyLastTenDays`; for seeded `CaseReport1.LastName` and `CaseReport1.City` values holding `fi`, under patterns carrying the ligature `ﬁ` in its place, `_` and sets beside it, `NUL`, the soft hyphen and `😀` inside the literal, and a combining acute or an accented letter, with the native result deciding each; and for the grid `q` on the same seeded texts under `Latin1_General_100_CI_AS`. Those tests only read: records with such characters in their values are created through the API, as every test record is, by `ListAndSearchTests`. At the real limit it shows sets longer than 4,000 units, one member repeated, negated and distinct members, returning the rows of native `LIKE` of the deduplicated set. `ListAndSearchTests` finds records with 4,001- to 4,700-unit patterns through the API, among them a 4,235-unit pattern repeating `😀`, which finds what native `LIKE` of the pattern holding it once finds under the rehearsal database's collation, a 4,002-unit pattern holding `ae` and its upper case, which find a record storing `æ` exactly where native `LIKE` of their first 3,999 characters does, and a marker's first 33 characters before a set of its 34th repeated 4,096 times finding the records native `LIKE` of the one-member set finds. One difference from one `LIKE` remains, recorded under "Parity gaps": a pattern holding a `[...]` set longer than 4,000 units that has no closing `]`, whose meaning depends on the collation, or that no such cut divides, as in a chain of ranges `[a-a-…-a]`, is a 400 under the field's canonical name, after the form's metadata is read and before any Find statement is sent. Legacy's inlined ANSI literal is `varchar`, whose 8,000 bytes hold 8,000 single-byte characters: measured on the seeded columns, 4,001 `%` returns 226 rows on `CaseReport1.LastName`, `a` × 4,000 followed by `%` and 8,000 `%` run on `CaseReport1.LastName` and on the `ntext` `FoodHistory3.BuyLastTenDays`, and 8,001 characters fail with error 8152. Compared with an `nvarchar` column, that literal is converted to `nvarchar(4000)` and loses every character past the 4,000th without an error: measured, `a` × 4,000 followed by `b` matches a stored `a` × 4,000 in `nvarchar(4000)` and `nvarchar(max)` alike.
- **Find admission**: at most two Find statements of the API process run at once. The store builds one record repository, registered once per process, and its `SearchAsync` takes one of two slots (`FindAdmission` in `src/EpiInfo.Data/Records/SqlRecordRepository.cs`) before it opens its connection, and gives it back once its statement has completed, whatever the outcome. While both slots are taken a search waits, holding no connection and sending nothing, until one comes free or its request is aborted. The wait has no deadline and no response of its own, so a search is never refused for want of a slot, and a 503 from Find always comes from SQL Server or its connection. Every admitted search runs its statements under `Database:CommandTimeoutSeconds`, so slots keep coming free; a client that gives up aborts its request, as the web client does at its 60-second request deadline (`REQUEST_DEADLINE_MS` in `web/src/api/client.ts`), and its waiting search then ends with no response and nothing sent. The value bound and the search-wide bounds ("Value bound" and "Search-wide bounds" in "Target choices") cap the alternatives and the pattern text of one statement, and the command timeout its run; the slots cap how many such statements occupy SQL Server workers together, so concurrent searches cannot take every worker that saves and record reads need. Measured 2026-11-27 through the API on a copy of the rehearsal database holding the records of earlier test runs, with one `LastName` criterion of 3,080 `%a<i>%` alternatives (32,766 units) that takes about 3.1 s alone: 4 concurrent searches each answered 200, in two pairs, at 3.1 s and 6.2 s, and `/health` answered 200 in 2 ms during the load. Measured 2026-11-27 through the API on a copy of the rehearsal database holding the records of earlier test runs, with one `LastName` criterion of 3,080 `%a<i>%` alternatives (32,766 units) that takes about 2.7 s: 8 concurrent searches each answered 200, in four pairs, at 2.8, 5.4, 8.2 and 11.0 s; 32 concurrent searches each answered 200, in 16 pairs about 2.7 s apart, the last at 43.4 s, so the last 8, admitted after waiting more than the 30-second command timeout, were answered as well, and none was a 503; one more search, whose client gave up after 5 s while it waited, ended with no response and one Information log line for the client's abort, and the pairs kept their pace after it; and `/health` answered 200 in at most 3 ms throughout. `FindAdmissionSqlRecordRepositoryTests` (`tests/EpiInfo.Data.Tests/Records/SqlRecordRepositoryTests.cs`) pins searches still waiting, with no connection opened and nothing sent, three times past a one-second command timeout; their admission in turn when one slot is given back, which shows that a failed search gives its slot back; and the cancellation of a waiting search, which takes no slot.
- **Not limited**: equality (`= @p`) and the 255-character multiline comparison (`SUBSTRING(col, 1, 255) = SUBSTRING(@p, 1, 255)`) have no length bound but their value's ("Value bound" in "Target choices"); a 32,767-character equality runs, and so do two 32,767-character plain multiline values on two fields. Phone Number, `GlobalRecordId`, numbers, dates, checkboxes, options and `RecStatus` have no length bound of their own either.

### Collation

- **Database collation**: `SQL_Latin1_General_CP1_CI_AS`, read with `DATABASEPROPERTYEX(DB_NAME(), 'Collation')` by direct SQL at write-up. `testdata/sql/00_create_database.sql` pins no collation, so the server default applies.
- **Final run**, 2026-11-26, on the run tree: the Compose `sqlserver` service's `EpiRehearsal`, straight after `db-init`, has the collation `SQL_Latin1_General_CP1_CI_AS` and compatibility level 160, read from `sys.databases` at run time, so the Compose server's default is the same collation.
- **Implied behaviour**: Find's equality and `LIKE` compare under that collation, case-insensitively and accent-sensitively, exactly as legacy does on this database.
- **`ListAndSearchTests`**, which reads the collation and pins that behaviour: pass in the final run, 2026-11-26, on the run tree, `dotnet test EpiInfo.Web.sln` on that database: 26 passed, and the test result of `FindEquality_FollowsTheDatabaseCollation`, the run tree's name of the collation test, records the standard output `Database collation: SQL_Latin1_General_CP1_CI_AS`, as it does in the rerun and the `Integration` run, and as it did in the final runs of `d450f367` and the 13:11Z run's tree. On the merged tree ("Changed after the run"): pass, 2026-11-26, 29 passed, on a restored copy of the rehearsal database whose nine `99_verify` checks showed `ok = 1`. `Find_FollowsTheDatabaseCollation`, which replaces that test, writes `Database collation: SQL_Latin1_General_CP1_CI_AS` to the test output and checks that `CaseReport1.LastName` carries that collation. It assumes no collation and reads no meaning into the name: each expected result is direct SQL shaped as Find selects it (active base rows `INNER JOIN`ed to every page table, `LastName = @p` or `LastName LIKE @p` with no `COLLATE` clause), and Find must return exactly those records for an equality on the marker, a `*` pattern on its first sixteen characters (`LIKE 'prefix%'`) and the same pattern with `_` in place of its hyphen. In the marker's own case each must find its record through Find and through direct SQL. Upper-cased, all three find the lower-case record here, through both alike. Before the merge, the same test also passed on scratch databases created with `Latin1_General_100_CS_AS` and `Latin1_General_100_BIN2` and loaded from `testdata/sql`, where the upper-cased criteria find nothing through either. `GridQ_MatchesTheMarkerInAnyCaseAndAsAnInnerSubstring` shows the grid `q` matching upper-case, lower-case and inner-substring variants of a marker. Those matches alone would also hold under the case-insensitive database collation, so the test checks the one list statement `SqlCommandRecorder` captured for each `q`, which pages and counts the filtered set: it compares all six `CaseReport` grid columns as `CONVERT(nvarchar(max), column) COLLATE Latin1_General_100_CI_AS LIKE @p COLLATE Latin1_General_100_CI_AS ESCAPE '\'`, holds no other `LIKE`, and carries the `q` text only as the bound `%q%` parameter; a blank `q` sends no `LIKE`. Removing either `COLLATE` from the grid predicate fails the test on any database collation.

## Parity gaps

Thirteen gaps were recorded, the thirteenth after the run. The six in P0 behaviour, the Legal Values options, Find wildcard patterns longer than 4,000 code units, repeated field names, the date display and entry mask, sign-only number entry and fixed-width text columns, are also reported as failing criteria: SC-5, SC-6 and SC-8 ("Run results"). The seven others are in P1 check code and the P2 permissions, which decide no P0 criterion. Four are in the check-code `=` operator (`src/EpiInfo.CheckCode/Evaluation/ValueComparer.cs`); the first also covers `NOT`'s comparison with `"true"` (`Evaluator.cs`). `EvaluatorTests` pins all four; for the fourth it pins only what holds under every desktop culture, that a date never equals date-only text, and the invariant form the server uses in place of the desktop's date format. No seeded program reaches any of them: the seeded comparisons are `Sex = "1"`, `FeverTemp = (.)` and `Fever = (-)`, and the one seeded `NOT` negates the comparison `FeverTemp = (.)`, a boolean (`testdata/sql/03_forms.sql:metaViews`). The fifth is in the Legal Values options; `SqlMetadataReaderTests` pins it, and no seeded field reaches it. The sixth is in `EPIWEEK`'s reading of date text (`src/EpiInfo.CheckCode/Functions/EpiWeekFunction.cs`); `EpiWeekFunctionTests` pins it, and no seeded program reaches it, because the one seeded call passes the Date field `OnsetDate`. The seventh is a check-code condition with no value (`Evaluator.cs`); `EvaluatorTests` pins it, and no seeded program reaches it, because every operand of a seeded `IF`, `AND` or `NOT` is a comparison. The eighth is a Find wildcard pattern longer than 4,000 code units: legacy shortened it, the target matches it whole, and it is a 400 only when it holds a `[...]` set longer than 4,000 units that is unclosed or that no cut keeping its members divides; a long literal run, of such a pattern or of a long grid `q`, is sought by 33 of its prefixes (`src/EpiInfo.Data/Records/SearchSqlBuilder.cs`, `src/EpiInfo.Domain/Search/SearchCondition.cs`, `src/EpiInfo.Domain/Search/SearchCriteriaParser.cs`). `SearchCriteriaParserTests`, `ListAndSearchBoundsTests`, `SearchStatementBoundsTests` (`tests/EpiInfo.Data.Tests/Records/SqlRecordRepositoryTests.cs`) and `ListAndSearchTests` pin it, and no seeded value needs such a pattern. The ninth is a field name repeated on a form (`src/EpiInfo.Domain/Metadata/FormDefinitionFactory.cs`); `FormDefinitionFactoryTests`, `RecordValidatorTests`, `SqlRecordRepositoryTests` and `SqlMetadataReaderTests` pin it, and no seeded form has one. The tenth is the date display and entry mask of an enabled date field (`web/src/components/fields/DateInput.tsx`); `FormView.test.tsx` and `FieldEffects.test.tsx` pin the native date control and the masked read-only display, and every writable seeded date field reaches it. The eleventh is a denied principal for P2 permissions (`src/EpiInfo.Data/Permissions/SqlPermissionReader.cs`); `SqlPermissionReaderTests`, `PermissionTests`, `WritePermissionSqlTests`, `FormView.test.tsx` and `useRecordEditor.test.ts` pin everything but a real denial, and every form reaches it, because the seeded login holds every permission. The twelfth is sign-only number entry (`web/src/components/fields/NumberInput.tsx`); no test pins it, and every writable seeded Number field reaches it. The thirteenth is short text on an `nchar(n)` column, which the target, like legacy, stores padded and reads back padded, so trailing-space variants store one value and a short legal choice reads back as a text that is no option (`src/EpiInfo.Data/Sql/ColumnValueConverter.cs`); `FieldTypeMapperTests`, `RecordValidatorTests`, `StoredValueConversionTests` and `ReservedWordIdentifierTests` pin it, no seeded column reaches it, because every seeded character column is `nvarchar` or `ntext`, and it is reported under SC-6.

### `=` on two texts: the desktop culture

- **Legacy:** two strings are trimmed and compared with `StringComparer.CurrentCultureIgnoreCase` (`Epi.Core.EnterInterpreter/Rules/Rule_CompareExp.cs:224-227`), so under the desktop's current culture with Windows NLS collation. `NOT` makes the same kind of comparison: it compares its operand's string form, untrimmed, with `"true"` using `StringComparison.CurrentCultureIgnoreCase` (`Epi.Core.EnterInterpreter/Rules/Rule_NotExp.cs:40`).
- **Kept:** the same trimming and a linguistic comparison that ignores case, with the invariant culture, so the answer does not depend on the server; `NOT` compares its operand's text, untrimmed, with `"true"` in that comparison (`src/EpiInfo.CheckCode/Evaluation/Evaluator.cs`, `IsTrueForNot`). The two agree on case, trimming, canonically equivalent forms (a precomposed `é` equals `e` followed by a combining acute accent) and ignorable characters. So `NOT` of `tr` + soft hyphen (U+00AD) + `ue` is false, as in legacy, while `IF` and `AND`, which legacy and the port both test by an ordinal comparison of the lowered form with `"true"` (`Rule_If_Then_Else_End.cs:170`; `Rule_AndExp.cs:41-44`), find that text not true.
- **Why not equal:** the server can run neither the desktop's culture nor NLS. The comparisons differ where NLS treats an expansion as equal, such as `ß` and `ss` (`"straße" = "STRASSE"` holds in legacy and not here), where a desktop culture has its own casing rules, such as the Turkish dotted and dotless `i`, and, for `NOT`'s comparison with `"true"`, wherever NLS and the invariant culture give a character a different collation weight. No seeded program compares such text, and none applies `NOT` to text.

### `=` on numeric text: the desktop culture

- **Legacy:** text against a number is parsed with `double.TryParse(string)` (`Epi.Core.EnterInterpreter/Rules/Rule_CompareExp.cs:123-149, 150-175`), whose styles are `NumberStyles.Float | NumberStyles.AllowThousands`, under the desktop's current culture.
- **Kept:** the same number styles, thousands separators included, with the invariant culture: `,` groups digits and `.` is the decimal point, as en-US reads them, so `"1,000" = 1000` and `"1,000.5" = 1000.5` hold. A result that is not finite is rejected, as .NET Framework rejected an overflow.
- **Why not equal:** the server cannot know the desktop's culture. Under a culture that uses `,` as the decimal separator, such as de-DE, legacy reads `"1,5"` as 1.5 and `"1.000"` as 1000, where the server reads 15 and 1.

### `=` with a boolean on the left that legacy cannot convert

- **Legacy:** with a boolean on the left and a non-empty value of another type on the right, the right side's string form is converted with `ConvertStringToBoolean` (`Epi.Core.EnterInterpreter/Rules/Rule_CompareExp.cs:182-185`; `Epi.Core.EnterInterpreter/EnterRule.cs:64-89`). Only `(+)`, `YES`, `Y`, `TRUE`, `T`, `(-)`, `NO`, `N`, `FALSE` and `F` convert, in any letter case and untrimmed. Any other text, and every number, date or option index, converts to `null`, and the cast of that `null` throws. The exception leaves the whole event block, so its remaining statements do not run; it is logged when errors are suppressed and raised otherwise (`Epi.Core.EnterInterpreter/Rules/Rule_Begin_After_Statement.cs:33-47`; `Rule_Begin_Before_Statement.cs:35-49`; `Rule_Begin_Click_Statement.cs:34-48`).
- **Kept:** the ten spellings convert exactly as in legacy, so `Fever = "No"` holds for an unchecked checkbox and not for a checked one. Every other right side gives one `error` diagnostic, `Type mismatch: a boolean can be compared only with a yes or no value.`, with the statement's line and command. That statement is skipped, the effects of earlier statements stand and the next statement runs, as for `EPIWEEK` without a date.
- **Why not equal:** a field event reports a runtime error as a diagnostic that keeps earlier effects and never blocks a save; it has no counterpart for an exception that ends the block. No seeded program compares a checkbox on the left with anything but `(-)`.

### `=` between a date and text: the desktop culture

- **Legacy:** a date and text are compared by the string forms of both sides, untrimmed, with `StringComparer.CurrentCultureIgnoreCase` (`Epi.Core.EnterInterpreter/Rules/Rule_CompareExp.cs:191-217`). The date's form is `DateTime.ToString()` in the desktop's current culture, such as `1/15/2011 12:00:00 AM` under en-US.
- **Kept:** the string forms of both sides, untrimmed, compared linguistically with the invariant culture, ignoring case. The date's form is `DateTime.ToString()` of the date at 00:00:00 (C-15) in the invariant culture, `01/15/2011 00:00:00`, and the text's form is the text as stored.
